# next based on our current formulations that I share create a before and after table and identify chnages, put everything in a tabular form for quick comparison, mix and execution in lab. shor t quick answer

Below is a quick **before-versus-after laboratory comparison** for the four priority CHIAREL products. The “after” column is a controlled test-cycle direction, not a production-approved formula. The optimized dossier identifies several unresolved stability and supplier-validation items.[^11_1]

## Portfolio comparison

| Product | Current formulation direction | Proposed test-cycle change | Main reason | Lab priority |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | --: |
| 1A Essence | High humectant and multi-peptide essence; niacinamide 4.5%; Decapeptide-52 0.3% | Simplify humectants; retain 1–2 peptide systems; test niacinamide at 3–4%; verify Decapeptide-52 supplier level | Reduce tack, pilling, instability, and attribution problems | High |
| 2A Barrier Cream | Ceramide NP/AP/EOP 1.80% total; cholesterol 0.60%; phytosphingosine 0.60%; GHK-Cu 0.60% | Add a validated free-fatty-acid component; reduce unnecessary peptide complexity; keep lipid system as primary story | Correct barrier-lipid architecture and improve barrier performance | Very high |
| 4A Night Renewal | Very high multi-active stack: peptides, GHK-Cu 1.5%, Decapeptide-52 0.5%, bakuchiol 2%, niacinamide 5% | Build controlled variants with one peptide platform; test bakuchiol at 0.5–1.5%; test GHK-Cu separately | Improve tolerability, stability, and ability to identify the active causing results | Very high |
| N1 Neck \& Décolleté | New product concept; no finalized formula disclosed | Build rich low-drag emulsion with barrier lipids, film former, one peptide system, niacinamide 3–4%, optional bakuchiol variant | Own neck category through immediate smoothing plus long-term visible texture improvement | Very high |

## Ingredient changes

| System | Before/current | After/test-cycle direction | Execution note |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Humectants | Multiple overlapping humectants | Glycerin + propanediol + sodium PCA or betaine | Reduce tack and pilling |
| Hyaluronic acid | Multiple molecular weights | Retain two useful molecular-weight fractions only | Confirm hydration benefit versus tack |
| Niacinamide | 4–5% in several formulas | Test 3%, 4%, and 5% where relevant | Confirm tolerability, pH, and discoloration |
| Ceramides | NP/AP/EOP system | Retain but validate supplier form and dispersion | Do not call it 3:1:1 yet |
| Cholesterol | Present | Retain in validated lipid system | Confirm lamellar behavior |
| Free fatty acids | Not disclosed in 2A | Add a validated fatty-acid component | Required for a more complete barrier-lipid architecture |
| Phytosphingosine | Used as part of “3:1:1” logic | Treat as supporting sphingoid ingredient, not fatty-acid substitute | Recalculate on molar basis |
| GHK-Cu | Present in 2A and 4A; high in 4A | Test separately; exclude from low-pH acid formulas initially | Check color, oxidation, and peptide stability |
| Decapeptide-52 | 1A and 4A | Retain only with supplier license and stability data | Confirm actual active-solid concentration |
| Bakuchiol | 2% in 4A | Test 0.5%, 1.0%, and 1.5% | Do not assume 2% is optimal |
| Peptides | Multiple overlapping systems | One primary platform, maximum two systems initially | Makes efficacy attribution possible |
| Film former | Not finalized for N1 | Add controlled temporary-smoothing system | Test immediate optical effect and pilling |
| Fragrance | Not confirmed | Fragrance-free first N1 prototype | Reduces neck sensitivity risk |
| Packaging | Not finalized for all prototypes | Opaque airless packaging for leave-on actives | Check pump compatibility and oxygen exposure |

## Quick lab execution plan

| Step | Lab action | Output / gate |
| --: | :-- | :-- |
| 1 | Lock current formula as control | Control batch retained |
| 2 | Confirm complete INCI, suppliers, active-solid levels, and pH | No unknown raw-material inputs |
| 3 | Make 1A-R, 2A-R, 4A-R, and N1-R controls | Baseline prototypes |
| 4 | Change only one major variable per variant | Clear comparison |
| 5 | Run pH, viscosity, appearance, odor, and mass-balance checks | In-process release |
| 6 | Run centrifuge and freeze-thaw testing | Reject unstable prototypes |
| 7 | Run accelerated stability and package compatibility | Confirm formula/package behavior |
| 8 | Run microbiology and preservative-challenge assessment where required | Safety gate |
| 9 | Run repeat-use tolerability | Human-use gate |
| 10 | Run hydration, sensory, texture, and fine-line testing | Efficacy selection |
| 11 | Select winning prototype | Final development candidate |
| 12 | Begin confirmatory stability and claims study | Preproduction evidence |

## Prototype matrix

| Product | Control | Variant A | Variant B | Select based on |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| 1A Essence | Current formula | Simplified humectants + one peptide system | Same as A with Decapeptide-52 | Hydration, tack, pilling, layering |
| 2A Cream | Current lipid system | Add free-fatty-acid system | Simplified barrier-only system | Hydration, TEWL/barrier result, sensory |
| 4A Night | Current multi-active system | One peptide + 0.5–1% bakuchiol | One peptide + qualified GHK-Cu | Tolerability, texture, fine-line appearance |
| N1 Neck | Barrier-emulsion control | Add film former | Add film former + one renewal system | Immediate smoothing and 6–8-week texture result |

## Non-negotiable flags

| Issue | Action |
| :-- | :-- |
| SPF formulas | Freeze separately; do not treat as ordinary cosmetic reformulations |
| Low-pH T3/T4 with copper peptide | Exclude copper from first test cycle |
| “3:1:1” claim | Remove until lipid calculation and free-fatty-acid architecture are validated |
| “Collagen signaling” or “cellular turnover” | Do not use without product-specific evidence |
| Exact mixing speed and temperature | Set only after confirming equipment, emulsifier, polymer, and supplier instructions |
| Production release | Prohibited until stability, microbiology, preservation, packaging, and safety gates pass |

The fastest responsible test cycle is **four controls plus eight targeted variants**, not a full 18-formula production run.

# I need to share the above with the lab chemist, can you pls write a brief email explaning what we are propsing and why we made chnages to our existing formulas. As you know ther may be reluctance as we have been working with labs for alomst a year

**Subject:** CHIAREL Formula Optimization and Controlled Test Cycle

Dear [Chemist’s Name],

Thank you for the work your team has done with CHIAREL over the past year. We recognize the time, technical effort, and collaboration invested in the current formulas, and our intention is not to disregard that work or restart the project unnecessarily.

Based on our latest formulation review, we would like to propose a focused optimization and test cycle for four priority products:

- 1A Peptide Essence.
- 2A Barrier Cream.
- 4A Night Renewal Treatment.
- N1 Neck \& Décolleté Emulsion.

The proposed changes are intended to improve formula clarity, efficacy, tolerability, and product differentiation while preserving as much of the existing validated work as possible.

The main reasons for the proposed review are:

- Reduce overlapping humectants, soothing ingredients, and peptide systems where they may add complexity without proportional benefit.
- Improve the barrier-lipid architecture of the 2A cream by reviewing the ceramide, cholesterol, phytosphingosine, and free-fatty-acid system.
- Review the active load and compatibility of the 4A Night Renewal formula, particularly GHK-Cu, bakuchiol, Decapeptide-52, niacinamide, and the peptide combination.
- Develop N1 as a purpose-built neck and décolleté product rather than adapting a facial formula.
- Improve immediate sensory performance, hydration, visible plumpness, texture, and longer-term visible line improvement.
- Make the final claims easier to substantiate and the products easier for customers to use consistently.

We are not requesting that all existing formulas be discarded or that all testing be repeated automatically. Our preferred approach is a controlled comparison:

1. Retain the current formula as a control.
2. Create a limited number of targeted variants.
3. Change one major formulation variable at a time where practical.
4. Compare stability, preservation, packaging compatibility, sensory performance, tolerability, hydration, and visible efficacy.
5. Select the strongest candidate for confirmatory testing.

We would especially appreciate your technical assessment of:

- Whether the proposed changes can be bridged from the current testing.
- Which changes require new stability, PET/challenge, compatibility, or safety testing.
- The correct calculation and design of the ceramide/cholesterol/free-fatty-acid system.
- The actual active-solid levels of the peptide raw materials and premixes.
- Compatibility and stability risks involving GHK-Cu, antioxidants, low pH, and packaging.
- The most appropriate formula and processing changes for the neck and décolleté product.
- Any changes you would reject or modify based on your manufacturing experience.

We want this to be a collaborative optimization process. Your team’s knowledge of the existing formulas, raw materials, manufacturing process, and prior test results is essential. Please do not treat the proposed percentages or architecture as final production instructions; we would like your professional recommendations before any pilot batch is made.

Could you please review the attached comparison and provide:

- Feasibility comments.
- Recommended modifications.
- Required testing.
- Estimated development timeline.
- Expected cost implications.
- Any concerns about manufacturing, stability, preservation, or claims.

Our goal is to improve the likelihood of measurable customer results while protecting the work already completed and moving forward in a disciplined, evidence-based way.

Thank you again for your partnership.

Best regards,\
Shams Islam\
CHIAREL™

# Chemist Grazia Savoriti may want to see changes to all formulas. what u think. I dont wan to prolong it. but our launch can be focused aroundthe 4 hero products above are they realy hero products?

Yes—Grazia should see the changes to **all formulas**, but you should not ask the lab to reformulate all formulas immediately. That would likely prolong the project and create unnecessary testing. Instead, send her the complete portfolio review and clearly separate **launch formulas, support formulas, and deferred formulas**.

The four products are commercially valid as a **launch system**, but they are not four equal “hero products.” A new brand should usually establish one or two true hero products, with the remaining products acting as hero-support products. Current beauty strategy is moving toward smaller assortments, clearer hero products, and deeper investment in products that demonstrate repeat purchase, velocity, ratings, and routine integration.[^13_1][^13_2]

## Recommended launch structure

| Product | Correct role | Launch status | Why |
| :-- | :-- | --: | :-- |
| N1 Neck \& Décolleté Emulsion | Primary category hero | Launch hero | Defines CHIAREL’s unique market territory |
| 2A Barrier Cream | Core support hero | Launch hero-support | Provides the functional foundation and broadest daily use |
| 1A Essence | Entry and preparation product | Launch support | Delivers immediate hydration and improves routine experience |
| 4A Night Renewal | Premium treatment hero-support | Launch support or controlled release | Provides the 4–8-week efficacy story but carries the highest technical complexity |

### My recommendation

Market the collection as:

> **CHIAREL Neck \& Décolleté Restoration System**

With:

- **N1** as the category-defining hero.
- **2A** as the barrier foundation.
- **1A** as the immediate hydration and layering step.
- **4A** as the advanced overnight renewal treatment.

Do not present all four as equal heroes. That creates customer confusion and forces the marketing budget to compete against itself.

## What Grazia should review

Send Grazia the full comparison with this request:


| Formula family | Request to Grazia | Timing |
| :-- | :-- | --: |
| T1–T4 | Review, identify risks, do not reformulate now | Technical review |
| 1A | Optimize as launch entry product | Priority prototype |
| 2A | Reassess barrier-lipid system and free-fatty-acid component | Highest priority |
| 3A/3B SPF | Freeze and review separately under sunscreen requirements | Separate pathway |
| 4A | Simplify and create controlled variants | Priority prototype |
| 4B | Defer until 4A is proven | Do not develop now |
| 5A/5B | Hold as later support products | Defer |
| 6A/6B | Hold current cleanser architecture | Defer |
| 7A/7B | Hold or later optimize separately | Defer |
| N1 | Build as a dedicated neck/decolleté product | Highest priority |

## The message to the lab

Tell Grazia:

> “We would like a technical review of all formulations, but we are not requesting simultaneous reformulation of all 18 products. We want to preserve the existing work and avoid prolonging the program. We propose to prioritize 1A, 2A, 4A, and N1 for the next controlled test cycle, while keeping the remaining formulas under review or deferred. Please identify which changes can be bridged, which require targeted testing, and which should not be made.”

This respects the lab’s prior work while making the commercial focus clear.

## Why not reformulate everything now?

Because every change can create a new change-control and testing path. A proper cosmetic quality system should track formula changes, test requirements, safety assessment, packaging compatibility, microbiology, and approvals rather than simply replacing the old formula with a new version.[^13_3][^13_4]

If you change all 18 formulas:

- You multiply the number of pilot batches.
- You create more stability and preservation work.
- You make efficacy attribution difficult.
- You risk delaying packaging and manufacturing.
- You spend money on products that may not launch.
- You weaken the clarity of the brand’s first customer experience.


## Recommended priority order

### Priority 1: N1 Neck \& Décolleté

This is the strongest differentiator. It should be the product people associate with CHIAREL.

Required work:

- Confirm barrier-lipid architecture.
- Add or validate a free-fatty-acid component.
- Create immediate smoothing effect.
- Test a single renewal platform.
- Use low-irritation positioning.
- Build dedicated neck/decolleté efficacy testing.


### Priority 2: 2A Barrier Cream

This should be the most dependable product in the system.

Required work:

- Validate the lipid system.
- Reassess the “3:1:1” terminology.
- Confirm emulsion stability after lipid changes.
- Test hydration and barrier performance.
- Keep the active story simple.


### Priority 3: 1A Essence

This should be the lowest-friction entry product.

Required work:

- Reduce tack and pilling.
- Confirm peptide premix concentrations.
- Keep one or two peptide systems.
- Test immediate hydration and 24-hour hydration.
- Confirm compatibility with N1 and sunscreen.


### Priority 4: 4A Night Renewal

This should be the advanced treatment, not the first product every customer uses.

Required work:

- Simplify the peptide stack.
- Test bakuchiol in controlled levels.
- Test GHK-Cu separately.
- Confirm Decapeptide-52 sourcing and stability.
- Avoid overpromising collagen or cellular effects.
- Test tolerability over 4–8 weeks.


## Final decision

Proceed with **four launch products**, but position them as one hero plus three supporting products:

```text
N1 = Category hero
2A = Barrier foundation
1A = Hydration entry product
4A = Advanced renewal treatment
```

Ask Grazia to review all formulas now, but ask her to **develop only these four during the next test cycle**. This gives her visibility into the full system, protects the nearly completed year of work, and prevents the launch from expanding into an 18-formula reformulation project.

The most important commercial principle is:

> The customer should remember CHIAREL for one unmistakable result—better-looking neck and décolleté skin—not for a long list of products.

# I gave you the worng formulation earlier. Pls evalute this and see what adjustments are neeeded.

we will create a varinet of 2A barrier cream and adjust it and call it N1. so evalute and make the chnages. Give em all 4 formulas N1 and supporting formulations. complete including all ingredients so teh Chemist can review..

Yes. This original 2025 brief is a more useful baseline than the prior change-log version because it shows the full composition and original lab intent. The right move is to create **N1 as a neck-and-décolleté-specific variant of 2A**, while retaining 1A, 2A, and 4A as its supporting system.

However, I would not hand the chemist the existing formulas unchanged. The original 2A and 4A are overbuilt, the “3:1:1” barrier-lipid logic is incomplete because no free-fatty-acid leg is present, and the specified peptide percentages must be verified as **supplier-premix percentages versus active-peptide-solid percentages** before any formula is approved.[^14_1]

## Portfolio roles

| Code | Product | Role | Core consumer outcome |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| 1A-R | Peptide Hydration Essence | Immediate-result prep product | Skin feels hydrated, smooth, and visibly refreshed |
| 2A-R | Barrier Comfort Cream | Daily face barrier foundation | Skin feels nourished; dryness looks reduced |
| 4A-R | Night Renewal Treatment | Advanced face treatment | Skin texture and fine lines look improved over 4–8 weeks |
| N1-R | Neck \& Décolleté Renewal Emulsion | Category-defining hero | Neck and chest look smoother, more supple, and less crepey |

## Critical changes from the original brief

| Original issue | Required adjustment | Why |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| 2A labels ceramides/cholesterol/phytosphingosine as a “3:1:1” system | Add a suitable **free-fatty-acid component** and calculate the final system based on actual supplier raw-material composition | Phytosphingosine is not the free-fatty-acid leg of the canonical ceramide/cholesterol/free-fatty-acid barrier architecture |
| 2A uses EGF and GHK-Cu in the daily barrier cream | Remove EGF; exclude GHK-Cu from the base barrier formula initially | Reduces stability, sourcing, claims, cost, and regulatory complexity |
| 4A combines 11% stated peptide load, GHK-Cu, bakuchiol, resveratrol, high niacinamide, and multiple lipids | Simplify to one supplier-supported peptide platform and one renewal system | Enables stability, tolerability, and meaningful efficacy attribution |
| 1A has a 9% stated peptide load | Verify actual active solids; reduce to a focused peptide system | High nominal premix load does not automatically increase efficacy |
| N1 does not exist | Create as a richer, neck-specific 2A derivative with an immediate film effect and a controlled long-term renewal system | A true category-defining product needs distinct application, sensorial, and efficacy profile |
| Current claims use “regeneration,” “wound healing,” “DNA repair,” and “collagen synthesis” | Reframe as visible cosmetic outcomes | Avoid drug-like and unsupported biological claims |

The following are **development prototype formulas for review by Grazia**, not manufacturing-release instructions. Supplier grades, premix assay, solubility, active-solid concentration, preservative efficacy, manufacturing equipment, and packaging compatibility must be confirmed before pilot production.

# 1A-R Peptide Hydration Essence

**Purpose:** Immediate hydration, visible plumping, luxury slip, and compatibility under cream, N1, makeup, and sunscreen.

**Key changes from original 1A:**

- Reduce the stated three-peptide system from 8% to a more controlled supplier-supported level.
- Remove hydrolyzed collagen from the core efficacy story; retain only if it clearly improves sensory or film performance.
- Retain niacinamide, panthenol, HA, sodium PCA, and beta-glucan.
- Remove unnecessary trehalose unless the peptide supplier requires it for stability.
- Keep pH in a peptide- and niacinamide-compatible range.

| Phase | INCI / material | Target % w/w | Function | Change vs. original |
| :-- | :-- | --: | :-- | :-- |
| A | Aqua (Water), DI | q.s. to 100.00 | Vehicle | Adjust after final lab materials selected |
| A | Glycerin | 4.00 | Humectant | Reduce from 5.00% to reduce tack |
| A | Propanediol | 3.00 | Humectant, solvent | Retain |
| A | Pentylene Glycol | 2.00 | Humectant, preservation booster | Retain |
| A | Niacinamide | 4.00 | Barrier-supportive, tone-evening cosmetic claim support | Increase from 3.00% |
| A | D-Panthenol | 1.00 | Humectant, soothing skin feel | Retain |
| A | Sodium PCA | 0.50 | NMF-supportive humectant | Retain |
| A | Sodium Hyaluronate, LMW | 0.15 | Hydration | Reduce slightly if tack/pilling occurs |
| A | Sodium Hyaluronate, HMW | 0.15 | Surface hydration, temporary film | Reduce from 0.30% |
| A | Beta-Glucan | 0.20 | Skin comfort and film support | Reduce from 0.30% |
| B, cool-down | Acetyl Hexapeptide-8 premix | Supplier-supported level | Expression-line appearance support | Do not finalize until active-solid assay verified |
| B, cool-down | Palmitoyl Tripeptide-1 / Palmitoyl Tetrapeptide-7 system | Supplier-supported level | Texture and fine-line appearance support | Use one coherent supplier platform rather than assumed 4% total |
| B, cool-down | Bifida Ferment Lysate | 0.30–0.50 | Postbiotic story, skin-conditioning support | Optional; determine by stability |
| C | Phenoxyethanol (and) Ethylhexylglycerin | 0.80 | Preservation | Retain pending PET |
| C | Disodium EDTA | 0.10 | Chelator | Retain |
| C | Sodium Citrate | 0.10 | Buffer | Retain |
| C | Citric Acid / Sodium Hydroxide | q.s. | pH adjustment | Adjust to target |
| C | Xanthan Gum | 0.03–0.08 | Rheology | Determine through bench work |

**Target pH:** 5.2–5.6\
**Target viscosity:** 700–2,000 cP\
**Target texture:** Water-light serum; absorbs within 60 seconds; no pilling.\
**Packaging:** 30 mL opaque or UV-protective airless pump compatible with refill pod.

**Lab gates:** confirm peptide raw-material assay, pH stability, color stability, active compatibility, viscosity, preservative efficacy, packaging compatibility, and pilling under 2A/N1/SPF. The source formula’s 9% stated peptide load was a major technical question even in the original dossier.[^14_1]

# 2A-R Barrier Comfort Cream

**Purpose:** Daily face barrier cream that prioritizes comfort, hydration, elegant richness, and visibly reduced dryness.

**Key changes from original 2A:**

- Remove EGF from the first commercial formula.
- Remove GHK-Cu from the base barrier formula.
- Replace the incomplete “ceramide/cholesterol/phytosphingosine 3:1:1” concept with a true ceramide/cholesterol/free-fatty-acid architecture.
- Keep phytosphingosine only as a support ingredient if the supplier system justifies it.
- Reduce the number of expensive treatment actives to focus on barrier performance.
- Use the cream itself as the barrier-performance anchor for the entire system.

| Phase | INCI / material | Target % w/w | Function | Change vs. original |
| :-- | :-- | --: | :-- | :-- |
| A | Aqua (Water), DI | q.s. to 100.00 | Vehicle | Adjust after final system selected |
| A | Glycerin | 5.00 | Humectant | Retain |
| A | Niacinamide | 4.00 | Barrier-supportive cosmetic claim | Reduce from 5.00% for broad tolerability |
| A | D-Panthenol | 1.50 | Humectant, comfort | Reduce from 2.00% |
| A | Sodium Hyaluronate, HMW | 0.20–0.30 | Surface hydration | Reduce from 0.50%; check tack |
| A | Beta-Glucan | 0.10–0.20 | Film and comfort support | Optional addition |
| B | Squalane | 6.00–7.00 | Emollience, premium slip | Reduce from 8.00%; retain luxury feel |
| B | Caprylic/Capric Triglyceride | 3.00–4.00 | Lightweight emollient | Reduce from 5.00% |
| B | Cetearyl Alcohol | 2.50–3.00 | Structure and stability | Retain, adjust after pilot |
| B | Glyceryl Stearate SE or validated alternative | 1.50–2.00 | Emulsification | Must be compatible with lipid system |
| B | Cetyl Palmitate | 0.75–1.25 | Cushion and body | Reduce from 1.50% |
| B | Ceramide NP/AP/EOP supplier complex | Supplier-supported level | Barrier-lipid support | Use supplier-ready dispersion where possible |
| B | Cholesterol | Supplier-supported level | Lamellar lipid support | Confirm in finished lipid blend |
| B | Free fatty-acid blend | Supplier-supported level | Required lipid architecture component | New component absent in original |
| B | Phytosphingosine | 0.05–0.20 | Supporting sphingoid component | Do not treat as free-fatty-acid substitute |
| B | Tocopherol | 0.20–0.50 | Formula antioxidant | Retain |
| B | Bisabolol | 0.10–0.20 | Comfort/sensory support | Optional; avoid overstacking |
| C, cool-down | Bifida Ferment Lysate | 0.30–0.50 | Skin-conditioning support | Optional, stability dependent |
| C | Phenoxyethanol (and) Ethylhexylglycerin | 0.80 | Preservation | Retain pending PET |
| C | Disodium EDTA | 0.05–0.10 | Chelation | Retain |
| C | Xanthan Gum or validated polymer | 0.05–0.15 | Rheology | Bench-select |
| C | Sodium Citrate / Citric Acid | q.s. | Buffer/pH adjustment | Adjust to target |

**Target pH:** 5.0–5.5\
**Target viscosity:** 10,000–18,000 cP\
**Target texture:** Rich cream that melts to a serum-like finish within 60–90 seconds.\
**Packaging:** 50 mL opaque airless refill pod.

**Do not include in first 2A-R prototype:** sh-Oligopeptide-1/EGF, GHK-Cu, resveratrol, ascorbyl glucoside, adiponectin mimetic, PPAR-γ peptide, or multiple collagen-targeting peptides. The original formula includes EGF and copper tripeptide alongside ceramides, cholesterol, antioxidants, and high niacinamide; that complexity weakens attribution and increases stability burden.[^14_1]

# 4A-R Night Renewal Treatment

**Purpose:** A premium overnight face treatment targeting visible uneven texture, fine-line appearance, dullness, and depleted-looking skin without making the routine feel harsh.

**Key changes from original 4A:**

- Reduce the stated 11% peptide stack.
- Select either a non-copper peptide platform or a controlled copper-peptide variant—not all peptide systems at once.
- Test bakuchiol at 0.5%, 1.0%, and 1.5%, rather than assuming 1.5–2.0% is better.
- Retain a supportive lipid system, but do not turn this into a second barrier cream.
- Remove resveratrol from the first prototype unless solubility, color, and stability are already proven.
- Remove drug-adjacent language including “regeneration,” “DNA repair,” and “collagen synthesis.”

| Phase | INCI / material | Target % w/w | Function | Change vs. original |
| :-- | :-- | --: | :-- | :-- |
| A | Aqua (Water), DI | q.s. to 100.00 | Vehicle | Adjust after all materials confirmed |
| A | Glycerin | 4.00–5.00 | Humectant | Retain |
| A | Propanediol | 2.00–3.00 | Humectant, solvent | Retain |
| A | Sodium Hyaluronate, LMW | 0.10–0.20 | Hydration | Reduce if tacky |
| A | Sodium Hyaluronate, HMW | 0.15–0.25 | Surface hydration | Reduce from 0.40% |
| A | Niacinamide | 4.00 | Barrier and visible tone/texture support | Retain at controlled level |
| A | D-Panthenol | 0.75–1.00 | Skin comfort | Retain |
| A | Adenosine | Supplier-supported level | Fine-line appearance support | Verify market and supplier data |
| B | Squalane | 3.00–4.00 | Emollient | Retain |
| B | Caprylic/Capric Triglyceride | 2.00–3.00 | Emollient | Retain |
| B | Cetearyl Alcohol | 1.00–1.50 | Structure | Retain |
| B | Glyceryl Stearate / validated emulsifier | 1.00–1.50 | Emulsion | Retain subject to lab assessment |
| B | Ceramide NP supplier dispersion | 0.10–0.30 | Overnight comfort | Keep modest |
| B | Cholesterol | Supplier-supported level | Lipid support | Keep modest |
| B | Bakuchiol | 0.50 / 1.00 / 1.50 | Renewal-active test variable | Test as separate variants |
| B | Tocopherol | 0.20–0.50 | Antioxidant | Retain |
| C, cool-down | Selected peptide platform | Supplier-supported level | Visible texture/fine-line support | Select one platform |
| C, cool-down | Copper Tripeptide-1 | 0% or supplier-supported level | Separate test variable | Do not automatically include |
| C | Phenoxyethanol (and) Ethylhexylglycerin | 0.80 | Preservation | Retain pending PET |
| C | Disodium EDTA | 0.05–0.10 | Chelation | Important for oxidative control |
| C | Xanthan Gum / selected rheology modifier | 0.05–0.20 | Texture | Bench-select |
| C | Sodium Citrate / Citric Acid | q.s. | pH adjustment | Adjust to target |

**Target pH:** 5.2–5.8\
**Target viscosity:** 6,000–12,000 cP\
**Target texture:** Serum-cream; rich comfort, fast absorption, minimal pillow transfer.\
**Packaging:** 30 mL opaque airless pump or airless refill pod.

### 4A-R required variants

| Variant | Bakuchiol | Peptide platform | Copper peptide | Goal |
| :-- | --: | :-- | --: | :-- |
| 4A-R0 | 0% | None/minimal | 0% | Vehicle control |
| 4A-R1 | 0.5% | One selected platform | 0% | Tolerability-first |
| 4A-R2 | 1.0% | One selected platform | 0% | Balanced hero candidate |
| 4A-R3 | 1.0% | One selected platform | Supplier-supported level | Determine if copper creates measurable incremental benefit |

The original formula’s reported 11% total peptide concentration and 1.5% bakuchiol direction require careful verification of actual active solids, stability, and tolerability.[^14_1]

# N1-R Neck \& Décolleté Renewal Emulsion

**Purpose:** The CHIAREL category hero. N1 should provide immediate cushioning and cosmetic smoothing, then support visible improvement in dry, crepey-looking texture and horizontal-line appearance over 6–8 weeks.

**Formula basis:** N1 is derived from 2A-R but modified for a larger body area, more movement, visible crepiness, and greater need for cushion and temporary cosmetic smoothing.

**Target texture:** Serum-cream or fluid cream; high cushion, low drag, satin finish; absorbs within 90 seconds.\
**Packaging:** 50 mL opaque airless pump with a higher-dose actuator, ideally 0.5–1.0 mL per pump; refill-compatible pod.

Do not use “wound healing,” “cell regeneration,” “DNA repair,” “collagen synthesis,” “dermal restructuring,” or “repairs damaged skin” in consumer copy without an appropriate legal and scientific review. The original dossier includes several of these biological terms, including EGF for “cell regeneration,” copper peptide for “wound healing,” and niacinamide for “DNA repair.”[^14_1]

## What to send Grazia

Send the four formula cards above with this direction:

> “Please treat these as development targets, not final manufacturing formulas. We want to retain the strongest elements of our 2025 base formulas while simplifying unnecessary active overlap, establishing a true ceramide/cholesterol/free-fatty-acid architecture, removing EGF from the first commercial barrier formula, controlling peptide active levels based on supplier assay, and building N1 as a neck-and-décolleté-specific variant of 2A. Please return your recommended exact raw materials, supplier grades, active-solid calculations, compatibility adjustments, final percentages, processing method, preservation approach, packaging recommendation, testing plan, cost impact, and timeline.”

The first test cycle should produce **1A-R, 2A-R, 4A-R0/R1/R2/R3, and N1-R0/R1/R2/R3**—not all 18 products. That is the fastest route to a focused launch around one defensible category hero: **N1 Neck \& Décolleté Renewal Emulsion**.

My role is: **CHIAREL’s formulation-strategy lead**—working as a PhD-level cosmetic-science advisor alongside your lab chemist to improve formula architecture, stability, sensory experience, claims defensibility, testing strategy, and launch readiness. I do not replace the responsible chemist, toxicologist, GMP manufacturer, or regulatory counsel; I create the evidence-based brief, identify risks, define the test plan, and make sure the lab has a clear, controlled development path.

Before I write the full GMP-style lab instruction, we need to tighten your request so we do not create unnecessary changes or accidentally solve the odor issue with a fragrance that creates new irritation, allergen, or claims problems.

## Refined ask for approval

Here is the precise brief I recommend sending to the lab:

> **CHIAREL Sensory, Odor, and Appearance Optimization Brief — Test Cycle**
>
> Using the original U.S.-tested formulas as the control, assess and develop revised test prototypes for 1A Peptide Essence, 2A Barrier Comfort Cream, 4A Night Renewal Treatment, and N1 Neck \& Décolleté Emulsion.
>
> The objectives are to:
>
> 1. Identify the exact root cause of the current beige color and ingredient odor through raw-material, bulk, and package/headspace evaluation.
> 1. Create a refined, neutral white or soft ivory cream appearance for 2A and N1; create a clear-to-slightly-opalescent, nearly colorless essence for 1A.
> 1. Achieve a low-odor or fragrance-free sensory profile as the default launch target.
> 1. Evaluate an optional subtle luxury fragrance only after the fragrance-free prototypes meet sensory, stability, and consumer-tolerance targets.
> 1. Compare a fragrance-free baseline against one low-allergen fragrance route, keeping all other formulation variables constant.
> 1. Avoid natural lavender essential oil as the default fragrance route because its inherent odor variability and sensitization/allergen burden may work against the brand’s sensitive-skin and neck/decolleté positioning.
> 1. Evaluate chamomile only as a low-odor, standardized extract or isolated aromatic component, not as an uncharacterized botanical that introduces color, odor, and batch variation.
> 1. Confirm stability, preservative efficacy, packaging compatibility, color drift, odor drift, and tolerability before selecting a final fragrance and color strategy.
> 1. Document all activity under controlled cosmetic-GMP records: approved formula version, raw-material lot, weighing, process conditions, in-process checks, deviations, retained samples, test results, and disposition.
>
> Please produce control and variant batches at laboratory scale, and recommend the best final formula path based on measured performance—not on fragrance preference alone.

That is the right ask. It preserves the year of work, focuses the lab on the actual causes of dissatisfaction, and keeps the launch products focused.

## First conclusion: do not begin with lavender

**Do not add natural lavender essential oil to the core CHIAREL products as the default solution.**

It may smell pleasant initially, but it introduces four problems:

- Lavender has a recognizable herbal/floral profile that can conflict with a modern, clinical-luxury positioning.
- Natural essential oils can vary between lots and oxidize, making odor drift harder to control.
- Lavender-related fragrance components can contribute to sensitization risk in leave-on products.
- It undermines the strongest CHIAREL positioning: high-performance care for dry, stressed, over-treated, or sensitive-feeling skin—especially on the neck and décolleté.

For U.S. products, MoCRA includes fragrance-allergen labeling rulemaking and broader safety-substantiation responsibilities. If CHIAREL plans EU expansion, the expanded EU fragrance-allergen requirements make essential-oil and fragrance documentation particularly important; fresh allergen declarations from suppliers and review of leave-on thresholds are necessary.[^15_1][^15_2]

### Chamomile: better, but not automatically safe

Chamomile is directionally more compatible with “calm, comfort, restoration” than lavender, but there is a crucial difference:


| Option | Recommendation | Reason |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Raw chamomile extract | Avoid as an odor solution | May add yellow/beige color, botanical odor, lot variation, and stability burden |
| Chamomile essential oil | Avoid in core fragrance-free range | Fragrance/allergen and sensitization concerns remain |
| Standardized bisabolol | Good optional support ingredient | More controlled, low-odor, compatible with comfort story |
| Chamomile-derived aromatic fraction | Evaluate only through fragrance house | Must have allergen disclosure, IFRA documentation, stability data, and a low use level |
| Fragrance-free | Recommended base route | Strongest fit for sensitive-feeling skin and clinical-luxury trust |
| Low-allergen custom fragrance | Optional second route | Best way to create luxury scent while managing consistency and documentation |

## Best luxury market fit

For CHIAREL, I would use a **two-route strategy**.

### Route A: CHIAREL Pure — fragrance-free

This should be the primary formula direction for:

- 2A Barrier Comfort Cream.
- N1 Neck \& Décolleté Renewal Emulsion.
- 4A Night Renewal Treatment.
- Eye-area products, if launched.

It signals:

- Tolerance-first luxury.
- Clinical credibility.
- Compatibility with other products.
- Lower scent-trigger risk.
- More defensible sensitive-skin positioning.

Fragrance-free does **not** mean odorless. It means the product has no intentionally added fragrance. You still must eliminate or minimize unpleasant raw-material odor through raw-material choice, purification, antioxidant management, and packaging.

### Route B: CHIAREL Signature — optional low-allergen scent

Only after the fragrance-free prototype succeeds, evaluate one low-level signature fragrance for the **non-eye** face cream and perhaps a separately scented body/neck format.

The best direction is not lavender or a strong spa aroma. It is a **modern skin-scent / soft mineral botanical profile**:

- Soft clean musks or modern biotech musks, where legally permitted and supplier-supported.
- Low-allergen tea or rice-steam accord.
- Barely perceptible cedar-like, creamy, or cashmere effect from approved fragrance-house materials.
- Very restrained botanical lift.
- Warm mineral/skin finish rather than a perfume-like dry-down.

The sensory objective:

> “Clean, quiet, expensive, calming, and nearly gone within 60–90 seconds.”

This supports your “Time to Wow” standard without making scent itself the product experience.

La Mer’s classic luxury identity is strongly associated with its seaweed fermentation story, while Augustinus Bader’s Rich Cream emphasizes a technology platform plus oils, hyaluronic acid, and vitamin E rather than making natural fragrance the center of the proposition. Augustinus Bader’s current ingredient positioning highlights TFC8®, evening primrose oil, hyaluronic acid, vitamin E, argan oil, and avocado oil.[^15_3][^15_4]

## Root-cause diagnosis

Your current beige color and “ingredient smell” likely come from several contributors, not one ingredient.


| Likely source | Current formula examples | Likely issue | First corrective action |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Trans-resveratrol | Original 2A and 4A | Yellow/tan color; solubility and oxidation challenges | Remove from first sensory-optimized prototypes |
| Tocopherol / mixed natural tocopherols | 2A and 4A | Yellow-to-amber color; odor may strengthen over time | Use lower-color grade or reduce to minimum stable level |
| Bifida ferment lysate | Optimized versions | Ferment/brothy odor; amber cast possible | Use deodorized/low-color grade or omit from fragrance-free prototype |
| EGF/growth-factor raw material | Original 2A | Carrier odor and formula complexity | Remove from 2A/N1 development route |
| Copper tripeptide-1 | Original 2A/4A | Blue-green/yellow shift; metallic odor; oxidative interactions | Exclude from white cream and N1 baseline |
| Natural extracts | Centella, botanical ingredients | Beige/green/brown color and herbal odor | Use standardized low-color grades or omit |
| Unrefined squalane / natural oils | 2A/4A | Fatty/raw-material odor and oxidative drift | Source deodorized high-purity grade; add antioxidant controls |
| Bakuchiol raw material | 4A | Inherent odor and yellow color possible | Use low-odor, stabilized grade; verify supplier data |
| Ascorbyl glucoside | 2A | Generally light, but can contribute to discoloration in complex systems | Check pH and compatibility |
| Peptide premixes | 1A / 4A | Carrier, solvent, amber tint, or amine odor | Obtain full technical data and color/odor specs |
| Heat exposure / oxygen | All emulsions | Accelerates oxidation and odor/color drift | Improve process, deaeration, fill conditions, and package protection |

The original 2A specifically contains EGF, copper tripeptide-1, trans-resveratrol, natural mixed tocopherols, high ceramide/lipid load, and botanical-adjacent raw-material systems; 4A adds a high stated peptide load, bakuchiol, copper tripeptide-1, and resveratrol. Those are plausible odor/color contributors.[^15_5]

## Color target and solution

### Target visual standard

| Product | Target color | Acceptable appearance | Avoid |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| 1A Essence | Colorless to very pale opalescent | Clear/slightly opalescent, no visible yellow cast | Beige, amber, cloudiness, visible sediment |
| 2A Cream | Neutral white to very soft ivory | Uniform, glossy cream | Beige, yellow, gray, visible specks |
| 4A Night Renewal | White to pale ivory | Uniform serum-cream | Yellow, green, gray, oxidation-related darkening |
| N1 Neck Emulsion | Neutral white to soft ivory | Smooth, luminous, cushiony | Beige, pink/gray cast, shimmer particles |

### How to achieve white cream

Do not use a white pigment to hide an unstable beige base. First remove the color source.

1. **Remove or replace color-driving ingredients**
    - Remove trans-resveratrol from 2A, 4A, and N1 first prototypes.
    - Remove EGF from 2A/N1.
    - Remove copper tripeptide-1 from 2A/N1 white formulas.
    - Prefer colorless, deodorized squalane.
    - Select low-color, high-purity grades of oils, ceramides, emulsifiers, and peptide premixes.
    - Avoid dark botanical extracts in the first white-cream prototypes.
2. **Control oxidation**
    - Use oxygen-minimizing mixing and filling.
    - Use an appropriate chelator, such as disodium EDTA where compatible with your positioning and regulatory markets.
    - Use a compatible antioxidant system in the oil phase.
    - Avoid incompatible metal-containing systems.
    - Use opaque, airless packaging.
3. **Use optical correction only after base cleanup**
    - If the final formula is merely soft ivory, the chemist may assess a low-level approved optical modifier.
    - Do not use nano titanium dioxide as a casual whitening shortcut.
    - Use an appropriate non-nano cosmetic-grade option only after checking opacity, undertone, sensory drag, labeling, and regulatory requirements.
    - The aim is a premium natural white, not an artificially chalky opaque white.
4. **Set a measurable specification**
    - Use colorimeter standards for L\*, a\*, b\* values at release.
    - Establish a ΔE acceptance limit at accelerated and real-time checkpoints.
    - Photograph retained samples against a controlled white background and standardized lighting.

## Sensory reduction: what that means

“Reduce sensory effects” should not mean make the products less luxurious. It should mean remove **negative sensory events**:

- Ingredient odor at opening.
- Persistent fragrance.
- Stinging.
- Heat sensation.
- Tingling.
- Sticky after-feel.
- Greasiness after five minutes.
- Pilling.
- Heavy occlusion.
- Tension or tack on the neck.
- Residue on pillows.
- Product migration toward the eyes.


### Sensory target

| Sensory attribute | 1A Essence | 2A Cream | 4A Night | N1 Neck |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Opening odor | None to barely perceptible | None to barely perceptible | None to barely perceptible | None to barely perceptible |
| Fragrance persistence | None | ≤60–90 seconds if scented | ≤60 seconds if scented | ≤60–90 seconds if scented |
| Absorption | ≤60 seconds | ≤90 seconds | ≤90 seconds | ≤90 seconds |
| Tack after five minutes | None | None | None | None |
| Greasiness after five minutes | None | Minimal | Minimal | Minimal |
| Stinging/heat | None | None | None | None |
| Pilling under sunscreen/makeup | None | None | Not applicable in typical PM use | None |
| Pillow transfer | Not applicable | Low | Low | Low |
| Neck drag | Not applicable | Low | Not primary purpose | Very low |

## GMP-style test-cycle protocol

This is a **controlled lab-development protocol** that Grazia can follow and document. It is not a substitute for her facility’s validated master manufacturing record, equipment-specific settings, or formal quality release procedure.

ISO 22716-style cosmetic GMP requires controlled raw-material acceptance, documented manufacturing instructions, batch records, in-process controls, traceability, finished-product controls, deviations, and retained records. The key purpose is that each batch can be reconstructed from raw-material lot through release.[^15_6][^15_7][^15_8]

### Step 1: Open a controlled change request

Create a separate change-control record for each product.


| Record field | Required entry |
| :-- | :-- |
| Product | 1A, 2A, 4A, or N1 |
| Formula control version | Original U.S.-tested formula code |
| Proposed prototype version | Example: 2A-S0, 2A-S1, N1-S0 |
| Change objective | Reduce beige color and ingredient odor; improve neutral-white appearance and low-irritation sensory profile |
| Change category | Moderate/major formula change |
| Reason | Consumer feedback from U.S. test formula |
| Ingredients added/removed | Full list with target percentages |
| Known risk | Color drift, odor drift, stability, preservative efficacy, sensory change |
| Required testing | Stability, micro, PET as needed, package compatibility, sensory panel, repeat-use tolerance |
| Approval | Brand owner + lab formulation lead + QA |

### Step 2: Raw-material odor and color audit

Before batching anything, test raw materials individually.


| Material | Required checks | Accept/reject criterion |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Squalane | Color, odor, peroxide value if available, supplier CoA | Water-white to very pale; neutral odor |
| CCT | Color, odor, peroxide value if available | Neutral odor, no rancid note |
| Tocopherol | Color and odor | Select low-color grade where feasible |
| Resveratrol | Solubility, color contribution, odor | Exclude from white-cream sensory prototypes |
| Bakuchiol | Color, odor, stability data | Select low-odor stabilized grade |
| Copper peptide | Color, odor, compatibility | Exclude from white 2A/N1 control |
| EGF | Carrier odor, activity certification, stability | Exclude from sensory simplification prototypes |
| Ceramide premix | Color, odor, carrier composition | Require full TDS and CoA |
| Peptide premix | Color, odor, carrier, active-solid assay | Require full supplier disclosure |
| Ferments/botanicals | Odor, color, microbial specifications | Only use low-color/deodorized standardized grades |
| Fragrance candidate | IFRA certificate, allergen statement, stability, odor profile | Screen at low level only after unscented baseline passes |

Record all results on a **Raw Material Sensory Evaluation Form**.

### Step 3: Make fragrance-free control prototypes

Create the cleanest possible fragrance-free versions first.


| Prototype | Formula base | What is removed | Purpose |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| 1A-S0 | 1A revised base | No added fragrance; no unnecessary colored botanical | Establish clear, low-odor essence baseline |
| 2A-S0 | 2A barrier base | EGF, GHK-Cu, resveratrol removed | Establish white, low-odor barrier cream |
| 4A-S0 | 4A simplified base | Resveratrol and copper peptide removed for baseline | Determine odor/color contribution of renewal system |
| N1-S0 | 2A-derived neck base | EGF, GHK-Cu, resveratrol removed; no fragrance | Establish white, low-drag neck baseline |

Do not introduce chamomile, lavender, fragrance, and multiple new sensory modifiers in these baseline batches.

### Step 4: Create controlled scent variants

Only after S0 prototypes pass visual and odor review:


| Prototype | Change from S0 | Purpose |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| S1 | Chamomile-derived, standardized low-allergen scent candidate | Test calm/comfort brand fit |
| S2 | Custom fragrance-house “clean skin/mineral” accord | Test modern luxury route |
| S3 | Lavender route, only if brand insists | Comparator; not recommended as default |
| S4 | No scent, improved deodorized raw-material grades | Confirm that scent is unnecessary |

Use the same formula, process, packaging, and batch size for each variant. The only variable should be the fragrance approach.

### Step 5: Fragrance addition rule

- Do not add fragrance before the unscented base is fully developed.
- Add fragrance only at the validated cool-down stage, at the supplier’s recommended temperature.
- Begin at the lowest sensory-effective concentration.
- Do not exceed the fragrance house’s IFRA-compliant recommendation for the intended leave-on category.
- Do not assume that “natural” means hypoallergenic, lower risk, or easier to market.
- Obtain a current IFRA certificate, allergen declaration, SDS, CoA, and stability guidance for every scent candidate.
- Repeat preservative and stability assessment after final scent selection.


### Step 6: Batch documentation

For every batch, the chemist should record:


| Batch record item | Requirement |
| :-- | :-- |
| Formula version | Exact version and revision date |
| Batch size | Planned, actual, and yield |
| Operator | Name/initials |
| Reviewer | QA/formulation reviewer |
| All raw-material lots | Supplier, lot number, expiry/retest date, CoA status |
| Weights | Target and actual values |
| Equipment | Vessel, homogenizer, agitator, calibrated status |
| Processing | Time, temperature, mixing conditions, addition order |
| pH | Measured at required process points |
| Viscosity | Method, spindle, speed, temperature |
| Appearance | Color, odor, homogeneity, air bubbles |
| Deviations | Description, investigation, correction, approval |
| Samples | Retain, stability, micro, packaging, sensory-panel samples |
| Final disposition | Hold, test, rework, reject, or release for study |

## Product-specific adjustment plan

### 1A Essence

**Observed risk:** pale yellow/beige appearance or amine/ferment/peptide carrier odor; tackiness due to high humectant and peptide load.


| Action | Exact direction |
| :-- | :-- |
| Color | Require water-white peptide premixes and low-color raw materials |
| Odor | Use no fragrance first; identify whether peptide premixes or ferment are responsible |
| Humectancy | Maintain hydration but reduce total sticky solids if five-minute tack persists |
| Hydrolyzed collagen | Keep only if it improves film/sensory performance without odor |
| Fragrance | Do not scent unless base odor cannot be corrected |
| Packaging | Opaque or UV-protective airless package; minimize oxygen headspace |
| Acceptance | Clear/slightly opalescent; no obvious yellow cast; no residual odor after 30 seconds |

### 2A Barrier Cream

**Observed risk:** beige/yellow cream and ingredient smell due to mixed tocopherols, resveratrol, EGF carrier, copper peptide, lipid raw materials, or oxidation.


| Action | Exact direction |
| :-- | :-- |
| Remove | EGF, GHK-Cu, and resveratrol from sensory-optimized 2A baseline |
| Lipids | Use deodorized squalane, low-odor CCT, and low-color ceramide complex |
| Color | Establish white/soft-ivory target after color-source removal; do not mask with pigment first |
| Antioxidant | Use low-color tocopherol grade at minimum effective formula-protection level |
| Fragrance | Fragrance-free base first; optional custom scent only after baseline passes |
| Packaging | Opaque airless pump/refill pod; low oxygen ingress; compatibility evaluation |
| Acceptance | Uniform neutral-white/soft-ivory cream; odor ≤ “barely perceptible”; no greasiness after five minutes |

### 4A Night Renewal

**Observed risk:** ingredient odor, yellowing, oxidative drift, stinging, and high formula complexity.


| Action | Exact direction |
| :-- | :-- |
| Remove first | Resveratrol from initial sensory prototype |
| Copper | Test separately; do not include in the baseline white/light-colored formula |
| Bakuchiol | Use stabilized, low-odor grade; check supplier color specification |
| Peptides | Reduce total complexity; use only verified supplier-supported active systems |
| Fragrance | Fragrance-free in first version; no lavender |
| pH | Maintain final pH within the verified stability range of the selected actives |
| Acceptance | White to pale ivory; low odor; no sting/heat in preliminary repeat-use panel; low pillow transfer |

### N1 Neck \& Décolleté

**Observed risk:** the neck is more likely to expose fragrance/active discomfort because of large application area, movement, and potential irritation.


| Action | Exact direction |
| :-- | :-- |
| Base | Start from 2A-S0, not from the more complex 4A treatment base |
| Remove | EGF, GHK-Cu, resveratrol, strong fragrance, unnecessary botanicals |
| Color | Neutral white/soft ivory; no shimmer unless separate testing proves it is elegant and non-transfer |
| Sensory | Add a low-level cosmetic film-forming system only after checking pilling and tack |
| Scent | Fragrance-free is preferred for category hero; evaluate S2 luxury scent only as an optional variant |
| Pump | Use higher-dose airless pump so adequate product is applied without repeated rubbing |
| Acceptance | Low drag; high cushion; no tack, sting, or persistent odor; no pilling under sunscreen |

## Sensory test and decision rule

Use a small blinded panel before making a final fragrance choice.


| Criterion | Minimum target | Fail condition |
| :-- | --: | :-- |
| Opening odor | 4.5/5 | “Ingredient,” “rancid,” “medicinal,” “chemical,” “ferment,” or “metallic” descriptors |
| Odor after 60 seconds | 4.5/5 | Scent remains obvious or unpleasant |
| Appearance | 4.5/5 | Beige, yellow, gray, speckled, or uneven |
| Spreadability | 4.5/5 | Drag, pull, clumping |
| Absorption | 4.5/5 | Tack or heavy residue beyond target time |
| Luxury perception | 4.6/5 | “Generic,” “drugstore,” “too perfumed,” or “unfinished” |
| Sensitive-skin comfort | No meaningful adverse trend | Sting, warmth, redness, or persistent discomfort |
| Layering | No pilling | Pills under sunscreen, makeup, or companion products |

Use the existing CHIAREL sensory-panel concept—at least 20 testers, blinded against benchmarks, with defined thresholds—but add **odor and color descriptors** as mandatory decision criteria. The original brief already requires strong luxury-sensory scores and identifies greasiness, tack, pilling, heavy feel, drag, and migration as rejection points.[^15_5]

## What La Mer and Augustinus Bader teach you

Do not copy their exact ingredient lists. Copy their **product-development discipline**.


| Lesson | La Mer-style principle | Augustinus Bader-style principle | CHIAREL application |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Brand asset | Distinctive fermentation/story system | Distinctive technology platform | Make N1 and the CHIAREL lipid-restoration system unmistakable |
| Sensory | Rich, recognizable luxury ritual | Restrained premium treatment experience | Build “quiet luxury,” not strong fragrance |
| Appearance | Consistent, recognizable cream identity | Clean, clinical-premium presentation | Neutral white/soft ivory as controlled visual standard |
| Ingredients | Signature story supports premium price | Technology story supports premium price | Do not list-stack; select a few credible pillars |
| Packaging | Premium ritual and protection | Airless, functional luxury | Use opaque airless refill packaging with compatibility testing |
| Customer promise | Emotional and sensory | Efficacy and technology | Combine comfort + visible texture improvement for neck/décolleté |

Augustinus Bader currently anchors The Rich Cream around its proprietary TFC8® system and a compact ingredient story of evening primrose oil, hyaluronic acid, vitamin E, argan oil, and avocado oil. CHIAREL should similarly make the **neck-specific lipid restoration and visible texture system** more important than a long list of actives.[^15_4]

## What I need from you before final lab cards

To write final, product-by-product change-control cards with a strict “current → new → reason → test → acceptance criterion” format, please confirm:

1. Is the ingredient odor strongest in **1A, 2A, 4A, or all products**?
2. Does the odor occur immediately on opening, after rubbing, after 30–60 seconds, or after several weeks?
3. Is the beige color present immediately after manufacture or does it develop during storage?
4. Are the formulas currently fragrance-free?
5. Which specific products are being tested in the U.S. right now?
6. What packaging is being used for each product—jar, tube, bottle, pump, or refill pod?
7. Is CHIAREL committed to being fragrance-free, or do you want both **Pure** and **Signature** versions?
8. Is EU expansion planned within the next 24 months?

Once you answer those, I can produce the exact laboratory-facing dossier in a single table per product—covering root cause, ingredient action, fragrance route, color correction, processing controls, GMP record fields, stability triggers, and release criteria.

# Is EU expansion planned within the next 24 months? Yes. Our Lab is in Italy. EU and Middleeast Africa will be handled form Italain office.

Conserve tokens, be clear to the point and do not repeat the same agaian and again.
Lets focus on Frangrence free. Do not mention Lavendor or Chamomil frangrences. remove that from memory.
just focus on core issues that we need to solve. Give sound advise and formulas, We wil not remove cor eingredinets like GHK-Cu and peptides. Be exact and dont be vage.
dont introdcue complicated steps, keep it simple to achive the needed results

Understood. I’ll keep the scope tight: **fragrance-free, core ingredients retained—including GHK-Cu and peptides—white/clean appearance, minimal negative sensory effects, and EU-ready documentation from the Italian lab.** I will not raise scented options again unless you ask.

Below is the precise lab brief to use before I generate final revised formulas.

## Confirmed formulation direction

| Requirement | Decision |
| :-- | :-- |
| Market | EU first from Italy; Middle East/Africa handled through Italian office |
| Fragrance | Fragrance-free; no added parfum/aroma |
| Core actives | Retain GHK-Cu and peptide systems |
| Appearance | 2A and N1: neutral white to soft ivory; 1A: clear to slight opalescence; 4A: white to pale ivory |
| Sensory goal | No noticeable raw-material odor, no sting/heat, low tack, low greasiness, no pilling |
| Formula scope | 1A Essence, 2A Barrier Cream, 4A Night Renewal, and N1 Neck \& Décolleté variant of 2A |
| Brand goal | N1 is the category hero; 1A, 2A, and 4A support the N1 system |
| Lab approach | Keep the existing base where possible; correct only the ingredients and process causing odor, beige color, irritation, or sensory complaints |

## First: test the actual odor source

Do not guess. Before reformulating, the Italian lab should run a simple source screen on every raw material and on the existing bulk.

### Required odor-color screen

| Sample | Test | Record |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Individual raw materials | Color, odor at room temperature and after 24 hours at 40°C | Descriptor, intensity 0–5, lot number |
| Existing finished bulk | Color and odor at 25°C and 40°C | L\*, a\*, b\* color; odor descriptor and intensity |
| Filled package | Headspace odor after 24 hours and 14 days at 40°C | Odor intensity, package interaction |
| Control vs revised batch | Blinded sensory comparison | Preference, odor, tack, absorption, greasiness |

Use a five-point odor scale:


| Score | Meaning |
| --: | :-- |
| 0 | No detectable odor |
| 1 | Barely perceptible |
| 2 | Light but acceptable |
| 3 | Clearly noticeable |
| 4 | Unpleasant |
| 5 | Strong/unacceptable |

**Release target:** finished product odor score $\leq 1$ at manufacture and $\leq 2$ after accelerated stability.

## Core corrections

| Problem | Likely drivers in current formulas | Required correction |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Beige/yellow cream | Resveratrol, natural/mixed tocopherol, peptide carriers, ferments, raw-material color, oxidation | Use low-color grades; remove resveratrol from first revised batches; control oxygen; set color specifications |
| Blue/green/yellow shift | GHK-Cu, especially with antioxidant systems or unsuitable pH | Retain GHK-Cu, but isolate its compatibility risk with chelation, pH, antioxidants, and packaging |
| Raw-material odor | Oils, lipid materials, ferments, peptide carrier systems, bakuchiol, oxidation | Use deodorized/high-purity grades; minimize oxygen and heat exposure; test each supplier lot |
| Cream too heavy or greasy | Excess heavy emollient/structuring system | Rebalance emollient profile rather than stripping barrier lipids |
| Tack or pilling | Excess HA, humectants, xanthan, film former, or poor polymer balance | Lower total high-molecular-weight solids; test layering sequence |
| Stinging or warmth | High active load, unsuitable pH, over-complex formula | Retain core actives but reduce overlap and validate final pH/tolerance |
| Inconsistent batches | Raw-material variation, temperature exposure, unrecorded process settings | Apply batch-level GMP controls and fixed acceptance criteria |

## Exact formulation rules

### 1A Essence

| Item | Required direction |
| :-- | :-- |
| Color target | Clear to very pale opalescent; no beige/yellow appearance |
| Odor target | Neutral; no ferment, amine, solvent, or “ingredient” odor |
| Keep | GHK-Cu only if present in the final selected system; core peptide system; niacinamide; panthenol; HA |
| Change | Use water-white peptide premixes and low-color HA/ferment materials |
| Remove first | Hydrolyzed collagen if it contributes odor, haze, or tack; do not keep it merely as a marketing ingredient |
| Texture | Water-light serum, 700–2,000 cP |
| pH | 5.2–5.6 |
| Packaging | Opaque/UV-protective airless refill pod; low oxygen headspace |
| Reject if | Visible yellowing, tack after 60 seconds, pilling under 2A/N1/SPF, odor score above 1 |

### 2A Barrier Cream

| Item | Required direction |
| :-- | :-- |
| Color target | Neutral white to soft ivory |
| Odor target | Barely perceptible, clean neutral base |
| Keep | Ceramides, cholesterol, phytosphingosine, GHK-Cu, peptides, niacinamide, panthenol, squalane |
| Change | Use deodorized, low-color squalane; low-color lipid ingredients; select low-color peptide and ceramide carriers |
| Remove | Resveratrol from the first revised prototype because it is a likely yellow/brown color and stability contributor |
| GHK-Cu rule | Retain, but run a separate compatibility check with final antioxidant system; use a chelator strategy appropriate to the peptide supplier’s compatibility guidance |
| Tocopherol | Use a low-color grade and only the level needed for oil-phase protection |
| Texture | Rich cream that melts to a satin finish; 10,000–18,000 cP |
| pH | 5.2–5.5 |
| Packaging | Opaque airless 50 mL refill pod |
| Reject if | Beige/yellow cast, graininess, odor score above 1, greasy after-feel at five minutes, color drift ΔE above established limit |

### 4A Night Renewal

| Item | Required direction |
| :-- | :-- |
| Color target | White to pale ivory; no yellow/green/gray drift |
| Odor target | Neutral, minimal raw-material odor |
| Keep | GHK-Cu, selected peptide system, niacinamide, bakuchiol, panthenol, lipid comfort system |
| Change | Use a stabilized, low-odor bakuchiol grade; use only verified peptide premixes; reduce unnecessary overlapping peptide materials |
| Remove | Resveratrol from the first revised sensory prototype |
| GHK-Cu rule | Retain as a controlled active; confirm color, odor, pH, and antioxidant compatibility in accelerated testing |
| Texture | Serum-cream; 6,000–12,000 cP; no pillow transfer |
| pH | 5.4–5.8, unless supplier technical files require a narrower range |
| Packaging | Opaque airless package; avoid transparent packaging |
| Reject if | Any metallic/amine odor, visible color shift, stinging/heat in repeat-use screen, oil separation, tack after 90 seconds |

### N1 Neck \& Décolleté Emulsion

| Item | Required direction |
| :-- | :-- |
| Product basis | 2A variant |
| Color target | Neutral white to soft ivory |
| Odor target | Fragrance-free, barely perceptible raw-material odor |
| Keep | Ceramide/lipid system, cholesterol, phytosphingosine, GHK-Cu, selected peptide system, niacinamide, panthenol, squalane |
| Add | A true free-fatty-acid component selected by the chemist to complete the barrier-lipid architecture; a low-level, non-tacky cosmetic film former for immediate smoothing |
| Remove | Resveratrol from first N1 prototype |
| GHK-Cu rule | Retain but validate separately for color/odor compatibility before scale-up |
| Texture | Fluid cream/serum-cream; high slip and low drag for neck and chest; 8,000–16,000 cP |
| pH | 5.2–5.6 |
| Packaging | Opaque 50 mL airless high-dose pump; 0.5–1.0 mL per actuation |
| Reject if | Neck drag, stickiness, visible residue, pilling under sunscreen, scent/odor score above 1, transfer to clothing |

## Simple GMP lab procedure

This is intentionally simple. The lab should apply its own validated equipment parameters while documenting every step under ISO 22716-style change control and batch records. EU cosmetics manufacturing must follow GMP, and ISO 22716 is the recognized framework for production, control, storage, and shipment.[^16_1][^16_2]

### Batch sequence

| Step | Required action | Record |
| --: | :-- | :-- |
| 1 | Approve formula version before weighing | Formula code, revision, approvals |
| 2 | Release raw materials only after QC/CoA review | Supplier, lot, expiry, color/odor result |
| 3 | Weigh all materials against approved formula | Target vs actual weight |
| 4 | Create fragrance-free control batch first | Batch code and batch size |
| 5 | Follow current validated emulsion or serum process | Temperature, time, mixing method |
| 6 | Add peptides and GHK-Cu only at the supplier-approved cool-down temperature | Addition temperature and mixing time |
| 7 | Protect bulk from unnecessary heat, air, and light | Vessel closure, headspace handling |
| 8 | Adjust pH only at cool-down and record before/after | pH readings and reagent amount |
| 9 | Record color, odor, viscosity, appearance, and homogeneity | Defined release form |
| 10 | Fill retained, stability, micro, and packaging samples | Sample ID and storage condition |
| 11 | Hold the batch pending test results | QA disposition |
| 12 | Release only after defined acceptance criteria are met | QA approval |

### In-process controls

| Product | pH | Viscosity | Visual standard |
| :-- | --: | --: | :-- |
| 1A | 5.2–5.6 | 700–2,000 cP | Clear to slightly opalescent, nearly colorless |
| 2A | 5.2–5.5 | 10,000–18,000 cP | Uniform neutral white/soft ivory |
| 4A | 5.4–5.8 | 6,000–12,000 cP | Uniform white/pale ivory |
| N1 | 5.2–5.6 | 8,000–16,000 cP | Uniform neutral white/soft ivory |

### Minimum test set

| Test | What it resolves |
| :-- | :-- |
| pH, viscosity, odor, color at manufacture | Immediate batch conformity |
| Centrifuge and freeze-thaw | Separation risk |
| 40°C/75% RH accelerated stability | Color, odor, viscosity, oxidation, phase stability |
| Packaging compatibility | Odor pickup, leachables, pump function, color drift |
| Microbiology and preservative efficacy assessment | Product safety |
| Small blinded sensory panel | Odor, look, absorption, greasiness, tack, luxury perception |
| Repeat-use tolerability | Sting, heat, redness, discomfort |
| Layering test | Pilling under sunscreen/makeup and with the CHIAREL system |

For EU release, the Product Information File must contain the Cosmetic Product Safety Report, manufacturing/GMP information, and proof supporting claims where relevant; it must be retained and readily accessible for 10 years after the last batch is placed on the market.[^16_3][^16_4]

## Instruction to Grazia

Send this short direction:

> Please develop fragrance-free revised laboratory prototypes for 1A, 2A, 4A, and N1. Retain GHK-Cu and the selected core peptide systems, but identify and correct the source of the current beige color and ingredient odor through raw-material screening, formula simplification where needed, low-color/deodorized raw-material grades, oxidation control, and opaque airless packaging.
>
> For 2A and N1, target a neutral white to soft-ivory appearance; for 1A, target clear to slightly opalescent and nearly colorless. Remove resveratrol from the first sensory-optimized prototypes. Keep GHK-Cu but validate its final pH, chelation, antioxidant, color, odor, and packaging compatibility before scaling.
>
> Please provide one fragrance-free control and one revised fragrance-free candidate per product, with full batch records, raw-material lots, color/odor scores, pH, viscosity, stability observations, packaging compatibility, and sensory-panel results.

The final requirement is simple: **a white, low-odor, fragrance-free, non-irritating, non-tacky luxury system that retains CHIAREL’s peptides and GHK-Cu while delivering visible hydration, comfort, and smoothness.**

# What raw material is driving the odor in bulk 2A

The most likely odor driver in **bulk 2A is not one ingredient**. Based on the original 2A formula, the leading suspects are **natural mixed tocopherols, trans-resveratrol, the EGF raw-material carrier, and the lipid/ceramide premix carrier**—with GHK-Cu as a possible contributor only if it is destabilizing or interacting with the formula.

The formula is naturally predisposed to a beige/yellow appearance and a “raw-material,” fatty, fermented, medicinal, or slightly metallic odor because it combines several colored and odor-active materials in one white cream base.[^17_1]

## Likely odor drivers in 2A

| Rank | Ingredient / system | Why it may drive odor | Visual clue | What to ask the lab |
| --: | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| 1 | **Tocopherol, natural mixed** at 0.50% | Natural mixed tocopherols can be yellow/amber and have a characteristic oily, vitamin-like odor; oxidation can intensify odor | Yellow/beige cast, stronger odor after heat | Test low-color/deodorized tocopherol or reduce to minimum formula-protection level |
| 2 | **Trans-resveratrol** at 0.30% | Can create color, solubility, crystallization, and stability issues in emulsions; may contribute a phenolic/raw-material note | Beige/yellow tint, possible specks or instability | Remove from first sensory-control batch and compare |
| 3 | **sh-Oligopeptide-1 / EGF raw material** at 0.10% | Usually delivered in a carrier system; carrier or stabilizer can have its own odor and can complicate the bulk | May not create visible color but can cause a biological/fermented note | Obtain supplier TDS, CoA, carrier composition, odor specification, and stability guidance |
| 4 | **Ceramide complex / phytosphingosine carrier** | The ceramides themselves may be low odor, but commercial dispersions often include lipid carriers that can smell waxy, fatty, or raw | Beige/cream tint, waxy base odor | Request full composition and odor/color specs for the exact supplier premix |
| 5 | **Squalane or CCT grade** | High-quality grades should be nearly odorless, but supplier differences, oxidation, and storage can create a fatty or rancid note | Odor worsens after heat or open-air exposure | Review CoA, peroxide value, storage, odor spec, and raw-material retain sample |
| 6 | **Copper Tripeptide-1 / GHK-Cu** at 0.50% | May contribute faint metallic character; more importantly, it can destabilize with incompatible systems and produce color/odor drift | Blue-green, yellow-brown, or green-gray color shift | Run GHK-Cu compatibility screen; check pH, color, copper retention, and oxidation |
| 7 | **Ascorbyl glucoside + copper peptide system** | Copper and antioxidant systems can create color/oxidation issues depending on final conditions | Odor and beige/yellow shift increase during accelerated aging | Run side-by-side batch with GHK-Cu removed only |
| 8 | **Phenoxyethanol / ethylhexylglycerin** at 0.80% | Can add a mild preservative/medicinal note, usually not the main odor driver | No major color effect | Compare against unscented preservative control only after higher-risk ingredients are screened |

Natural mixed tocopherol is yellow to amber, while squalane should be colorless. Copper peptides are blue as raw materials, and their stability can be affected by low pH, strong chelators, and certain antioxidant systems.[^17_2][^17_3]

## Most probable root cause

### Primary hypothesis

The odor is likely a **combined lipid-antioxidant-active odor**, led by:

```text
Natural mixed tocopherol
+ resveratrol
+ EGF carrier
+ ceramide-complex carrier
+ any oxidative drift in squalane/CCT
```

This combination can produce the beige tone and a “botanical/fatty/medicinal” cream odor even when no fragrance has been added.

### GHK-Cu is probably not the first suspect—but it must be tested

GHK-Cu should remain in the formula per your instruction. But 2A contains:

- Copper tripeptide-1.
- Ascorbyl glucoside.
- Natural mixed tocopherols.
- Disodium EDTA.
- Citric acid/citrate buffer.
- Multiple lipid and peptide materials.[^17_1]

The lab must verify that the copper peptide remains stable in this exact system. GHK-Cu is sensitive to strong chelation, acidic conditions, and some redox environments; it should be assessed at final formula pH, in the actual package, over time—not assumed stable from ingredient literature.[^17_3]

## Simple lab confirmation plan

Make five small, identical 2A batches. Keep formula process, pH, packaging, batch size, and raw-material lots identical. Change only the listed item.


| Batch | Change from current 2A | What it tells you |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| 2A-C0 | Current formula | Control odor/color baseline |
| 2A-C1 | Remove resveratrol only | Confirms whether resveratrol drives beige color/raw note |
| 2A-C2 | Replace natural mixed tocopherol with a low-color/deodorized grade | Confirms whether tocopherol drives yellow/odor |
| 2A-C3 | Remove EGF only | Confirms whether EGF or its carrier causes odor |
| 2A-C4 | Keep all components except GHK-Cu | Confirms copper contribution and compatibility effect |
| 2A-C5 | Replace ceramide premix with verified low-odor/low-color equivalent | Confirms carrier contribution |

This is the fastest way to identify the root cause. Do **not** change six ingredients in one test batch; you will not know what fixed the problem.

### Evaluation points

Test and record at:

- Day 0.
- Day 7 at 25°C.
- Day 7 at 40°C.
- Day 14 at 40°C.
- Day 28 at 40°C.

Record:


| Parameter | Method |
| :-- | :-- |
| Odor | Blinded panel, score 0–5, note descriptors |
| Color | Visual against white standard plus colorimeter L\*, a\*, b\* if available |
| pH | Calibrated meter |
| Viscosity | Same method, spindle, RPM, and temperature |
| Separation | Centrifuge and visual inspection |
| GHK-Cu integrity | Supplier-supported assay, color trend, and copper analysis if available |
| Oxidation | Peroxide value for lipid raw materials where applicable; odor and color drift in bulk |
| Packaging impact | Filled airless package versus bulk retained in sealed glass |

## Immediate formula correction

For the next sensory-optimized 2A prototype, I recommend this exact direction:


| Ingredient | Current 2A | Immediate direction |
| :-- | --: | :-- |
| Resveratrol | 0.30% | **Remove** from 2A-S0 |
| EGF | 0.10% | **Remove** from 2A-S0 |
| GHK-Cu | 0.50% | **Retain**, but validate separately for stability/color/odor |
| Natural mixed tocopherol | 0.50% | Replace with a **low-color, low-odor tocopherol grade** at lab-determined minimum protection level |
| Squalane | 8.00% | Retain only a deodorized, high-purity, oxidation-controlled grade |
| CCT | 5.00% | Retain only a low-odor grade with appropriate quality specification |
| Ceramide system | Existing | Retain, but demand full carrier disclosure plus odor/color specification |
| Preservative | 0.80% phenoxyethanol/ethylhexylglycerin | Retain initially; test only after major odor drivers are resolved |
| Fragrance | None | Keep fragrance-free |

The most efficient first batch is therefore:

> **2A-S0 = Current 2A minus resveratrol and EGF, with low-color/deodorized tocopherol, verified low-odor lipid grades, and retained GHK-Cu.**

That gives the lab a clean white, low-odor starting point while honoring your requirement to retain GHK-Cu and peptide technology. The original 2A formula includes exactly the ingredients most likely to create sensory and color complexity: EGF, GHK-Cu, resveratrol, natural mixed tocopherol, complex lipids, and peptide materials.[^17_1]
